On the Open TS/ST Correspondence
Authors: Matijn François, Alba Grassi
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
The topological string/spectral theory correspondence establishes a precise, non-perturbative duality between topological strings on local Calabi-Yau threefolds and the spectral theory of quantized mirror curves. While this duality has been rigorously formulated for the closed topological string sector, the open string sector remains less understood. Building on the results of Marino and Zakany (J Phys A 50:325401, 2017. 10.1088/1751-8121/aa791e, JHEP 05:014, 2019. 10.1007/JHEP05(2019)014) and François and Grassi (Ann Henri Poincaré 26:2117, 2025. 10.1007/s00023-024-01469-4), we make further progress in this direction by constructing entire, off-shell eigenfunctions for the quantized mirror curve from open topological string partition functions. We focus on local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0, whose mirror curve corresponds to the Baxter equation of the two-particle, relativistic Toda lattice. We then study the standard and dual four-dimensional limits, where the quantum mirror curve for local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0 degenerates into the modified Mathieu and McCoy–Tracy–Wu operators, respectively. In these limits, our framework provides a way to construct entire, off-shell eigenfunctions for the difference equations associated with these operators. Furthermore, we find a simple relation between the on-shell eigenfunctions of the modified Mathieu and McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator, leading to a functional relation between the operators themselves.

Introduction

The topological string/spectral theory (TS/ST) correspondence [1–4] establishes a precise non-perturbative relation between the partition functions of topological string theory on local Calabi-Yau (CY) threefolds and the spectral properties of certain quantum mechanical operators on the real line. These quantum operators are obtained through the quantization of mirror curves [5, 6] and correspond to Baxter equations for a class of relativistic integrable systems, such as cluster integrable systems [7, 8] or elliptic Ruijsenaars-Schneider (RS) systems [9].

The TS/ST correspondence itself is structured in two parts: one relating closed strings to the spectrum, and the other relating open strings to the eigenfunctions. A central feature of this duality is the relationship between the string coupling constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s$$\end{document}gs and the reduced Planck constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ given by1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g_s = \frac{4\pi ^2}{\hbar } \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gs=4π2ħ.This implies that string perturbation theory naturally encodes non-perturbative effects on the spectral theory side. Conversely, the usual WKB expansion in spectral theory gives us the non-perturbative effects on the topological string side. Thus, the TS/ST correspondence bridges perturbative expansions in one theory with non-perturbative phenomena in its dual counterpart. This allows for the derivation of exact, closed-form expressions for many quantities on both sides of the correspondence. Another important consequence of (1.1) is the existence of the so-called self-dual, or maximally symmetric point [10], given by1.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hbar = 2 \pi = g_s \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ħ=2π=gs.At this special point, the TS/ST correspondence predicts remarkable simplifications, not only on the string theory side but also in operator theory, see e.g. [1–4, 11, 12].

In this work, we focus on the example where the underlying CY geometry is local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_0$$\end{document}F0. In the closed string sector, one important statement of the TS/ST correspondence is [3]1.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det \left( 1+\kappa \rho \right) =\sum _{k \in {\mathbb {Z}}}\textrm{e}^{\textrm{J}(\mu + \textrm{i}2 \pi k, \xi , \hbar )} \, , \qquad \qquad \kappa =\textrm{e}^{\mu } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det1+κρ=∑k∈ZeJ(μ+i2πk,ξ,ħ),κ=eμ,where the \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ is the so-called “mass” parameter, associated with the residue of the CY one-form at infinity. The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(\mu ,\xi ,\hbar )$$\end{document}J(μ,ξ,ħ) represents the topological string grand potential, incorporating both perturbative and non-perturbative contributions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s= 4 \pi ^2 / \hbar $$\end{document}gs=4π2/ħ, see (3.9). From the perspective of the moduli space, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(\mu ,\xi ,\hbar )$$\end{document}J(μ,ξ,ħ) is defined around the large radius point, and its perturbative part in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s$$\end{document}gs is given by the standard Gopakumar-Vafa (GV) free energy, see (3.17) and (3.19). The summation over k in (1.3) effectively smooths all the singularities across the closed string moduli space, parametrized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, allowing one to move away from the large radius point. Indeed, we can prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ is a trace class operator on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\mathbb {R}})$$\end{document}L2(R) [13, 14], and therefore its spectral determinant on the left-hand side of (1.3) is an entire function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. We can expand it around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}κ→∞, the large radius point, or around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = 0$$\end{document}κ=0, the orbifold point [3, 4, 15]. Let us also note that the summation over k in (1.3) is also essential for ensuring good modular properties of the determinant [10, 16] and for connecting it with q-isomonodromic \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ-functions [17–27].

The main motivation of this work is to extend the relation in (1.3) to the open string sector. To this end, the first step is to define the open string grand potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ), where x is the open string modulus. This was done in [1, 2] by focusing on the case where the brane is inserted in the outer leg of the toric diagram, the explicit form is given in (3.22), (3.31). Analogous to the closed string sector, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) incorporates both perturbative and non-perturbative contributions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s = 4 \pi ^2 / \hbar $$\end{document}gs=4π2/ħ and is defined in the large radius frame. However, one limitation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) is that it is not an entire function of the open string modulus x, which is a desirable property for a background-independent, non-perturbative formulation of open strings [28]. It was further suggested in [1, 2] that this requires a combination of the form1.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi (x, \kappa ) = \sum _{k \in {\mathbb {Z}}} \sum _{\sigma } \textrm{e}^{\mathrm{J_\sigma }(x, \mu + \textrm{i}2 \pi k, \xi , \hbar )} \, , \qquad \qquad \kappa =\textrm{e}^{\mu } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ(x,κ)=∑k∈Z∑σeJσ(x,μ+i2πk,ξ,ħ),κ=eμ,where the summation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ is expected to play a role analogous to the sum over k in (1.3), but for the open string modulus x. In particular, just as the sum over k smooths out all singularities in the closed string moduli, the sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ should similarly ensure that (1.5) becomes an entire function of x.

In this paper, building on insights from [1, 2, 29], we make this expectation precise by providing an explicit form for this summation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ in the case of local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_0$$\end{document}F0, at generic values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ and the complex moduli \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ. Specifically, we find that the precise combination to consider is1.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi (x, \kappa )= \sum _{k \in {\mathbb {Z}}}\left( \textrm{e}^{ \textrm{J}(x, \mu + \textrm{i}2 \pi k, \xi , \hbar )} + \textrm{e}^{\frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2}+\frac{\pi x}{\hbar }+ \textrm{J}(-x-\textrm{i}\pi , \mu + \textrm{i}\pi + \textrm{i}2 \pi k, \xi , \hbar )} \right) \, .\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ(x,κ)=∑k∈ZeJ(x,μ+i2πk,ξ,ħ)+eiħπ22+πxħ+J(-x-iπ,μ+iπ+i2πk,ξ,ħ).From the perspective of spectral theory, the combination (1.6) is a solution of the functional difference equation corresponding to the quantized mirror curve (1.4),1.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ( x + \textrm{i}\hbar , \kappa ) + \psi (x-\textrm{i}\hbar ,\kappa ) + 2 \textrm{e}^{2\xi } \cosh (x) \psi (x,\kappa ) +\kappa \psi (x,\kappa )=0 \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ(x+iħ,κ)+ψ(x-iħ,κ)+2e2ξcosh(x)ψ(x,κ)+κψ(x,κ)=0.Difference equations of the type (1.7) admit many solutions. The proposal (1.6) stands out in three ways. Firstly, (1.6) is always a well-defined function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x, \kappa , \xi $$\end{document}x,κ,ξ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ solving (1.7), and not just a formal solution.1 Secondly, (1.6) becomes a proper eigenfunction of the operator (1.4) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ is a root of the spectral determinant (1.3). Thirdly, the eigenfunctions (1.6) are entire in x for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \in \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}κ∈C. We do not have a complete, mathematical proof for these statements, but we performed many analytic and numerical tests that support them.

This paper is organized as follows. In Sect. 2, we analyse the matrix model (1.10) in some detail and show how its canonical transformation (1.8) naturally leads to the symmetric structure of the two contributions in (1.6). In Sect. 3, we translate this into a conjecture for the eigenfunctions in terms of open topological strings, as given in (1.6), and we perform several detailed tests of the proposal. In Sect. 4, we examine two specific limits of our construction: the standard four-dimensional limit [30, 31], defined in (4.1), and the dual four-dimensional limit [17, 32], defined in (4.33). In the standard four-dimensional limit, the difference equation (1.7) reduces to the Fourier-transformed Mathieu operator whose eigenvalue equation reads1.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sqrt{t} \left( \phi (x + \textrm{i}\epsilon , E)+\phi (x-\textrm{i}\epsilon , E)\right) +{x}^2\phi (x, E)- E \phi (x, E)=0 \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tϕ(x+iϵ,E)+ϕ(x-iϵ,E)+x2ϕ(x,E)-Eϕ(x,E)=0,where t, E and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ correspond to the four-dimensional limits of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ in (1.7), respectively. As with (1.7), this equation has many formal solutions. However, our construction identifies a special class of eigenfunctions that are entire, even off-shell. These are given by1.12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi (x, E)= \phi _{1}\left( \frac{x}{\epsilon },\frac{\sigma }{\epsilon },\frac{t}{ \epsilon ^4}\right) +\phi _{2}\left( \frac{x}{\epsilon },\frac{\sigma }{ \epsilon },\frac{t}{\epsilon ^4}\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ(x,E)=ϕ1xϵ,σϵ,tϵ4+ϕ2xϵ,σϵ,tϵ4with1.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \phi _{2}(x, \sigma , t)=&\phi _{1}(-x, \sigma , t)\left[ \frac{ \textrm{e}^{- \frac{\textrm{i}}{2} \partial _\sigma F_\textrm{NS}^\textrm{4d}\left( \sigma , t \right) }\left( \textrm{e}^{2 \pi x} - \textrm{e}^{2 \pi \sigma } \right) - \textrm{e}^{\frac{\textrm{i}}{2} \partial _\sigma F_\textrm{NS}^\textrm{4d}\left( \sigma , t \right) } \left( \textrm{e}^{2 \pi x} - \textrm{e}^{- 2 \pi \sigma } \right) }{\textrm{e}^{2 \pi \sigma } - \textrm{e}^{-2 \pi \sigma }}\right] \, , \end{aligned}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ2(x,σ,t)=ϕ1(-x,σ,t)e-i2∂σFNS4dσ,te2πx-e2πσ-ei2∂σFNS4dσ,te2πx-e-2πσe2πσ-e-2πσ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi _{1}(x,\sigma ,t)$$\end{document}ϕ1(x,σ,t) is defined in (4.13) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ and E being related by (4.10). The factor in square brackets in (1.13) is crucial to ensuring that the off-shell function (1.12) is entire. When evaluated on-shell, this factor is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pm 1$$\end{document}±1 depending on the parity of the eigenfunction, and our result reproduces the well-known expression for the on-shell eigenfunction in terms of 2d/4d surface defects in the Nekrasov–Shatashvili (NS) phase of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω-background [33–41]. On the other hand, in the dual four-dimensional limit (4.33), the operator (1.4) leads to the McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator [17]1.14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\textrm{e}^{4 t^{1/4}\cosh \hat{x}}{ \cosh \left( \frac{\hat{y}}{2} \right) }\textrm{e}^{4 t^{1/4}\cosh \hat{x}}} \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e4t1/4coshx^coshy^2e4t1/4coshx^,whose eigenfunctions are computed by 2d/4d surface defects in the GV (or self-dual) phase of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω-background [29]. Even though the off-shell eigenfunctions of (1.11) and (1.14) are quite different, when evaluated on-shell, they are related in a remarkably simple way. This in turn provides a clear functional relation between the modified Mathieu (1.11) and McCoy–Tracy–Wu (1.14) operators, see Sect. 4.3 and equation (4.50). In Sect. 5, we conclude and outline some open problems. We also have four appendices that provide technical details and definitions necessary for understanding the results in the main text.

Eigenfunctions and Matrix Models

The spectral problem

The quantized mirror curve (2.2) is then a symmetric, strictly positive operator, defined on the intersection of the domains of the multiplication and difference operators. Hence, one can define its Friedrichs extension, see [13]. It is this self-adjoint extension we will consider in everything that follows, and we refer to it as the quantum mirror curve. This leads to a purely discrete spectrum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{E_n\}_{n \in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}{En}n∈N for the quantum mirror curve, see [3, 13, 14]. Integrating quasi-periodic functions

In the following sections, we frequently encounter integrals involving Faddeev’s quantum dilogarithm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _b$$\end{document}Φb. To compute these integrals, we will make extensive use of Lemma 2.1 from [45], which we briefly review for future reference. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: \mathcal {U} \rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}f:U→C be an analytic function with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U} \subseteq \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}U⊆C open, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {C} \subseteq \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}C⊆U an oriented path, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a \in \mathbb {C} \setminus \left\{ 0 \right\} $$\end{document}a∈C\0 a constant such that the following properties hold: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U} = a + \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U=a+U ,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z) \left( f(z + a) - f(z) \right) \ne 0$$\end{document}f(z)f(z+a)-f(z)≠0 for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathcal {C}$$\end{document}z∈C,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f(z+a) f(z-a) = f^2(z)$$\end{document}f(z+a)f(z-a)=f2(z) for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z \in \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}z∈U,then the following equality holds2.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _\mathcal {C} f(z) \textrm{d}z = \left( \int _\mathcal {C} - This will be the case for the integrals of interest to us.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U} = a + \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U=a+U ,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {U} = a + \mathcal {U}$$\end{document}U=a+U ,

The eigenfunctions in matrix models coordinates

The general construction

Off-shell eigenfunctions in the matrix model coordinates q, p were found in [1, sec. 2], following [46]. Let us define2.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi ^{\pm }(q; \kappa ) = E^{\pm }(q) \sum _{N=0}^{\infty } \left( \pm \kappa \right) ^N \Psi _N(q) \, , \qquad \qquad E^{\pm }(q) = \exp \left( \pm \frac{q}{\sqrt{2} b^2}\right) \texttt {f}(q) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ξ±(q;κ)=E±(q)∑N=0∞±κNΨN(q),E±(q)=exp±q2b2f(q),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt {f}$$\end{document}f given in (2.11) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _N(q)$$\end{document}ΨN(q) is defined by the following unnormalized expectation value2.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi _N(q) = \frac{1}{2^N N!} \int _{\mathbb {R}^N} \textrm{d}^N q \, \prod _{k = 1}^N \tanh \left( \frac{q - q_k}{\sqrt{2} b^2} \right) \, \texttt {v}\left( q_k \right) \prod _{\ell = k+1}^N \tanh ^2\left( \frac{q_k - q_\ell }{\sqrt{2} b^2} \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨN(q)=12NN!∫RNdNq∏k=1Ntanhq-qk2b2vqk∏ℓ=k+1Ntanh2qk-qℓ2b2,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\texttt {v}$$\end{document}v is defined in (2.16). Note that also (2.18) is entire in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, in parallel with (2.13). This follows again from the trace class property of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ.

Using (2.18), we can write the eigenvalue equation (2.9) as2.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^{\pm }\left( q + \textrm{i}\frac{\pi b^2}{\sqrt{2}} \right) + \Omega ^{\pm }\left( q - \textrm{i}\frac{\pi b^2}{\sqrt{2}} \right) = - \sqrt{2} \pi b^2 \kappa \texttt {v}(q) \Omega ^{\pm }(q, \kappa ) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω±q+iπb22+Ω±q-iπb22=-2πb2κv(q)Ω±(q,κ),where2.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^{\pm }(q, \kappa )= \exp \left( \pm \frac{q}{\sqrt{2} b^2}\right) \sum _{N=0}^{+\infty } \Psi _N(q) \left( \pm \kappa \right) ^N \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω±(q,κ)=exp±q2b2∑N=0+∞ΨN(q)±κN.At the level of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _N$$\end{document}ΨN, equation (2.20) reads2.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Psi _N\left( q + \textrm{i}\frac{\pi b^2}{\sqrt{2}}\right) -\Psi _N\left( q - \textrm{i}\frac{\pi b^2}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = \textrm{i}{\sqrt{2} \pi b^2} \texttt {v}(q) \Psi _{N-1}(q) \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨNq+iπb22-ΨNq-iπb22=i2πb2v(q)ΨN-1(q).Therefore, the spectral problem in the matrix model coordinates q, p can be formulated as follows. We look for solutions of (2.20) which are analytic in the strip \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \textrm{Im}{\left( q \right) } \right| < \pi b^2 / \sqrt{2}$$\end{document}Imq<πb2/2 and which belong to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\mathbb {R}})$$\end{document}L2(R). In the off-shell eigenfunctions (2.21), the first requirement is already implemented because of the specific form of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _N(q)$$\end{document}ΨN(q) given in (2.19), as we will verify in the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=1$$\end{document}N=1 below. As for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2({\mathbb {R}})$$\end{document}L2(R) requirement, we have2.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Omega ^{\pm }(q, \kappa ) \simeq {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \det (1\pm \kappa \rho ) \exp \left( \pm \frac{q}{\sqrt{2}b^2} \right) \quad & q \rightarrow + \infty \\ \det (1\mp \kappa \rho ) \exp \left( \pm \frac{q}{\sqrt{2}b^2} \right) \quad & q \rightarrow - \infty \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω±(q,κ)≃det(1±κρ)exp±q2b2q→+∞det(1∓κρ)exp±q2b2q→-∞leading to the quantization condition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \det (1+\kappa \rho )=0$$\end{document}det(1+κρ)=0 as expected and in agreement with the discussion in subsection 2.1. Hence, we can regard \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{\pm }$$\end{document}Ξ± as an analogue of the Jost functions in one-dimensional scattering theory [1, 46]: the functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi _{\pm }(q;\kappa )$$\end{document}Ξ±(q;κ) become genuine, square-integrable eigenfunctions of (2.9) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = -\exp (E_n) < 0$$\end{document}κ=-exp(En)<0 is on-shell,2.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Xi _{+}\left( q; -\textrm{e}^{E_n} \right) = (-1)^n \, \Xi _{-}\left( q;-\textrm{e}^{E_n} \right) \in L^2\left( \mathbb {R} \right) \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ξ+q;-eEn=(-1)nΞ-q;-eEn∈L2R.Their canonical transformation then yields the eigenfunctions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (x; -\textrm{e}^{E_n})$$\end{document}ψ(x;-eEn) of (2.7) in the topological string (x, y)-coordinates, as discussed in subsect. 2.4.

The case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 1$$\end{document}N=1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in \pi \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈πQ>0

One can then see that the integrand of (2.25) is quasi-periodic in the sense of the lemma in subsect. 2.2 when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^2 \in \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}b2∈Q>0: we have quasi-periodicity under shifts by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{i}\sqrt{2} \pi b^2 m = \textrm{i}\sqrt{2} \pi n$$\end{document}i2πb2m=i2πn when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^2 = n / m$$\end{document}b2=n/m with n, m positive coprime integers. The eigenfunctions in outer topological string coordinates

The general construction and symmetric structures

One motivation for considering the outer topological string (x, y)-coordinates is that they establish a direct connection with the open topological string in the presence of a D-brane on the external leg of the toric diagram, as we will discuss in Sect. 3. Consequently, in these coordinates, we obtain a particularly explicit framework for computing these eigenfunctions using topological string partition functions.2

The eigenfunctions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi (x,\kappa )$$\end{document}ψ(x,κ) in the topological string (x, y)-coordinates and the eigenfunctions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Xi (q,\kappa )$$\end{document}Ξ(q,κ) in the matrix model (q, p)-coordinates are then related by a canonical transformation. More precisely,2.40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^{\pm }(x,\kappa ) = \int _{\mathbb {R}} \textrm{d}q \, U\left( x, q \right) \Xi ^{\pm }(q,\kappa ) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ±(x,κ)=∫RdqUx,qΞ±(q,κ),where from [1]2.41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} U\left( x, q \right) = \frac{2^{1/4}}{\sqrt{2 \pi \hbar }} \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \left( \frac{x^2}{2} - \sqrt{2} \left( x - \xi \right) q + \frac{q^2}{2} \right) \right) \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ux,q=21/42πħexpiħx22-2x-ξq+q22.Hence, if we take the eigenfunctions (2.18), the corresponding eigenfunctions in topological string coordinates are2.42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^\pm (x, \kappa )=\sum _{N\geqslant 0}(\pm \kappa )^N \psi _{N}^\pm (x) \, , \qquad \qquad \psi _{N}^\pm (x) = \int _{\mathbb {R}} \textrm{d}q \, U\left( x, q \right) E^{\pm }(q) \Psi _N(q) \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ±(x,κ)=∑N⩾0(±κ)NψN±(x),ψN±(x)=∫RdqUx,qE±(q)ΨN(q).Note that this integral is only well-defined when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^{\pm }(q) \Psi _N(q)$$\end{document}E±(q)ΨN(q) is integrable. However, we have2.43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} E^{+}(q) \Psi _N(q)&\simeq {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \exp \left[ \left( \frac{b^2 - 2}{2 \sqrt{2} b^2} \right) (- q) \right] \exp \left( - \textrm{i}\frac{2 \sqrt{2}}{\pi } \frac{\xi q}{b^2} \right) \quad & q \rightarrow + \infty \\ \exp \left[ \left( \frac{b^2 + 2}{2 \sqrt{2} b^2} \right) q \right] \quad & q \rightarrow - \infty \end{array}\right. } \\ E^{-}(q) \Psi _N(q)&\simeq {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \exp \left[ \left( \frac{b^2 + 2}{2 \sqrt{2} b^2} \right) (-q) \right] \exp \left( - \textrm{i}\frac{2 \sqrt{2}}{\pi } \frac{\xi q}{b^2} \right) \quad & q \rightarrow + \infty \\ \exp \left[ \left( \frac{b^2 - 2}{2 \sqrt{2} b^2} \right) q \right] \quad & q \rightarrow - \infty \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned} \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E+(q)ΨN(q)≃expb2-222b2(-q)exp-i22πξqb2q→+∞expb2+222b2qq→-∞E-(q)ΨN(q)≃expb2+222b2(-q)exp-i22πξqb2q→+∞expb2-222b2qq→-∞.Hence, the unitary transformation (2.40) is well-defined only when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^2>2$$\end{document}b2>2, as also noted in [1]. This raises the question of how to make sense of the canonical transformation (2.42) when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar = b^2 \pi \leqslant 2 \pi $$\end{document}ħ=b2π⩽2π.3 Our strategy to address this issue is the following. In this region, we will momentarily set aside convergence issues and directly apply the Lemma from subsect. 2.2. This approach gives a finite result for any value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^2$$\end{document}b2, even if the starting point was problematic for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ b^2 \leqslant 2 $$\end{document}b2⩽2. The case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi =0$$\end{document}ξ=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^2=2$$\end{document}b2=2 was analysed in [1, p. 15] using the same strategy.

Let us first make some simple observation regarding the relation between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^+$$\end{document}ψ+ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^-$$\end{document}ψ-. Using the parity properties of the quantum dilogarithm (A.5) and the expression (2.19), we have2.44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} E^{\pm }(-q) \Psi _N(-q)&= \exp \left( \mp \frac{\sqrt{2} \pi }{\hbar } q \right) \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } 2 \sqrt{2} \xi q \right) E^{\pm }(q)\left( -1 \right) ^N \Psi _N(q) \\&= \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } 2 \sqrt{2} \xi q \right) E^{\mp }(q) \left( -1 \right) ^N \Psi _N(q) \, , \\ \psi _{N}^\pm (-x)&= \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2}\right) \exp \left( \mp \frac{\pi x}{\hbar } \right) \left( -1 \right) ^N \psi _{N}^\pm (x \mp \textrm{i}\pi ) \, , \\&= \left( -1 \right) ^N \psi _{N}^\mp (x) \, . \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E±(-q)ΨN(-q)=exp∓2πħqexpiħ22ξqE±(q)-1NΨN(q)=expiħ22ξqE∓(q)-1NΨN(q),ψN±(-x)=expiħπ22exp∓πxħ-1NψN±(x∓iπ),=-1NψN∓(x).The eigenfunctions behave then as2.45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Xi ^{\pm }(-q, \kappa )&= \exp \left( \mp \frac{\sqrt{2} \pi q}{\hbar } \right) \exp \left( \textrm{i}\frac{2 \sqrt{2}}{\hbar } \xi q \right) \Xi ^{\pm }(q, - Because of the simple relation between the two eigenfunctions, we will often work with2.46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi _{N}(x)\equiv \psi _{N}^+(x) \, , \qquad \qquad \psi (x) \equiv \psi ^+(x) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψN(x)≡ψN+(x),ψ(x)≡ψ+(x),and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{N}^-(x)$$\end{document}ψN-(x) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^-(x)$$\end{document}ψ-(x) can then simply be found from (2.44) and (2.45) respectively.

We are now going to study (2.40) and (2.42) in detail. First, it was conjectured in [1, 2] that this integral can be written as the sum of two contributions, which are in a one-to-one correspondence with the two saddles of the integrand on the right-hand side of (2.42). Second, in [29], a special scaling limit of (2.42) was analysed in detail, and it was found that the two saddles are related in a simple way. By combining these two observations, we can propose the following ansatz for the expression of the eigenfunctions in the topological string coordinates:2.47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi ^{ \pm }(x, \kappa ) = \omega ^{ \pm }(x, \kappa ) + \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2}\pm \frac{\pi x}{\hbar }\right) \omega ^{ \pm }( - x \mp \textrm{i}\pi , - \kappa ) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ±(x,κ)=ω±(x,κ)+expiħπ22±πxħω±(-x∓iπ,-κ),for some functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{\pm }(x,\kappa )$$\end{document}ω±(x,κ). Our result reads then2.48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { \psi (x, \kappa ) = \omega (x, \kappa ) + \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2}+\frac{\pi x}{\hbar }\right) \omega ( - x - \textrm{i}\pi , - \kappa ) \, , } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ(x,κ)=ω(x,κ)+expiħπ22+πxħω(-x-iπ,-κ),for some function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega (x,\kappa )$$\end{document}ω(x,κ). It is important to note that the parity relation for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi ^{\pm }(x,\kappa )$$\end{document}ψ±(x,κ) in (2.45) is the same as the one relating the two terms in (2.48), ensuring the self-consistency of our proposal (2.48). At the level of components in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ expansion (2.18), equation (2.47) becomes2.49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi _{N}^\pm (x)= \omega _{N}^\pm (x) + \left( -1 \right) ^N \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2}\pm \frac{\pi x}{\hbar }\right) \omega _{N}^\pm (-x \mp \textrm{i}\pi )\, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψN±(x)=ωN±(x)+-1Nexpiħπ22±πxħωN±(-x∓iπ),for some functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{N}^{\pm }(x)$$\end{document}ωN±(x) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{N}^{-}(x) = (-1)^N \omega _{N}^{+}(-x)$$\end{document}ωN-(x)=(-1)NωN+(-x). Equation (2.49) reads equivalently2.50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { \psi _{N}(x)= \omega _{N}(x)+ \left( -1 \right) ^N \exp \left( \frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2}+\frac{\pi x}{\hbar }\right) \omega _{N}(-x -\textrm{i}\pi )} \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψN(x)=ωN(x)+-1Nexpiħπ22+πxħωN(-x-iπ),for some function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _{N}(x)$$\end{document}ωN(x).

We will test this proposal in several ways and, we also give explicit expressions for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega $$\end{document}ω by using topological string theory, see subsect. 3.3. As we discuss in appendix C, the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi =0$$\end{document}ξ=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar =2\pi $$\end{document}ħ=2π analysed in [1, 2] is special and the above structure is hidden.

The case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 0$$\end{document}N=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in \pi \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈πQ>0

In this subsection, we compute \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\psi _{N} = \psi _{N}^+$$\end{document}ψN=ψN+ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 0$$\end{document}N=0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in \pi \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈πQ>0,4 which serves two goals. We are interested in2.51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \psi _0\left( x \right) = \int _\mathbb {R}\textrm{d}q \, U\left( x, q \right) E{\left( q \right) } \, , \qquad \qquad E{\left( q \right) } = E^+{\left( q \right) } \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ0x=∫RdqUx,qEq,Eq=E+q.It is important to note that the integrand in (2.51) is a meromorphic function of q, which inherits some quasi-periodicity from the quantum dilogarithms (A.7),2.52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{U{\left( x, q + \textrm{i}\sqrt{2} \pi k b^2 \right) } E{\left( q + \textrm{i}\sqrt{2} \pi k b^2 \right) }}{U{\left( x, q \right) } E{\left( q \right) }} = \textrm{e}^{- \textrm{i}\pi k^2 b^2} \textrm{e}^{-2 k \xi } \textrm{e}^{2 k x} \textrm{e}^{-\sqrt{2} k q} \textrm{e}^{ \textrm{i}\pi k} \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} k b^2} \nonumber \\ \prod _{\ell = 0}^{\left| k \right| - Following the lemma, we can rewrite (2.51) as2.55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \psi _0{\left( x \right) } = \left( \int _{\mathbb {R}+ \textrm{i}0} - \int _{\mathbb {R}+ \textrm{i}\sqrt{2} \pi n + \textrm{i}0} \right) \textrm{d}q \nonumber \\ & \quad \frac{U{\left( x,q \right) } E{\left( q \right) }}{1 - \left( -1 \right) ^{\left( n+1 \right) m} \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} n} \textrm{e}^{-2 m \xi } \textrm{e}^{2 m x} \textrm{e}^{- \sqrt{2} m q} \left( \frac{1 - \left( -1 \right) ^m \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} n} \textrm{e}^{2 m \xi } \textrm{e}^{\sqrt{2} m q}}{1 - \left( -1 \right) ^m \textrm{e}^{- \textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} n} \textrm{e}^{- 2 m \xi } \textrm{e}^{\sqrt{2} m q}} \right) } \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ψ0x=∫R+i0-∫R+i2πn+i0dqUx,qEq1--1n+1meiπ2ne-2mξe2mxe-2mq1--1meiπ2ne2mξe2mq1--1me-iπ2ne-2mξe2mq.The integrand above is a meromorphic function of q with a finite number of poles inside the integration contour and an essential singularity at infinity. Note furthermore from (2.43) that the integrand decays exponentially inside the whole contour for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Re}{\left( q \right) } \rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}Req→±∞. Hence, we can close the contour at complex infinity and the integral reduces to a sum over the residues of the poles.

To simplify the discussion, we will assume that either \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi \ne 0$$\end{document}ξ≠0 or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \in \left( 2 \mathbb {N}+ 1 \right) $$\end{document}n∈2N+1.6 There are potential poles of the integrand coming from E at (A.2)2.56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} q = \pm \sqrt{2} \xi \pm \textrm{i}\sqrt{2} \pi \left( \left( k + \frac{1}{2} \right) + \frac{n}{m} \left( \ell + \frac{1}{4} \right) \right) \, , \qquad \qquad k , \ell \in \mathbb {N}\, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}q=±2ξ±i2πk+12+nmℓ+14,k,ℓ∈N,and all the poles inside the integration contour in (2.55) are simple as a direct consequence of the observation made around (A.10). The ’t Hooft expansion

An important way to test the conjectured structure in (2.50) is by analysing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\psi _{N}^\pm \left( x \right) }$$\end{document}ψN±x (2.42) in a ’t Hooft limit. This limit was studied in detail in [1, sec. 3] for the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi = 0$$\end{document}ξ=0, see also [51]. We will closely follow their approach and use it to argue for the structure in (2.50). The ’t Hooft limit is defined by taking2.66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hbar , N, \xi , \left| q \right| , \left| x \right| \rightarrow + \infty \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ħ,N,ξ,q,x→+∞,while keeping the following ratio’s constant,2.67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lambda = \frac{N}{\hbar } \, , \qquad \quad \xi _D = \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } \xi \, , \qquad \quad q_D = \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } q \, , \qquad \quad x_D = \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } x \, ~. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}λ=Nħ,ξD=2πħξ,qD=2πħq,xD=2πħx.

The TS/ST Correspondence for Local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0

In this section, we first review some aspects of the TS/ST correspondence for the closed string sector and then discuss the generalization to the open string sector. We focus on the particular case where the toric CY threefold is local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0.

The quantum mirror map and Wilson loop

Mirror symmetry plays an important role in the TS/ST correspondence, with one of its key components being the quantum mirror map. Originally introduced from a geometrical perspective, this map was defined as the quantization of the A-period in the mirror curve [6, 53]. Subsequently, it was understood that, from a geometric engineering perspective, this map could be identified with a Wilson loop in a corresponding five-dimensional gauge theory, see [41, 54–56].

The quantum mirror map for local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_0$$\end{document}F0 is given in [6, sec. 7.2] or [57, eq. (3.58)]. The first few orders in a large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ expansion are3.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} t_B(\hbar )= 2 \mu - 2 \left( \textrm{e}^{4 \xi } + 1 \right) \textrm{e}^{-2 \mu } - \left( {3}\textrm{e}^{8 \xi } + 2 \left( \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } + 4 + \textrm{e}^{-\textrm{i}\hbar } \right) \textrm{e}^{4 \xi } + {3} \right) \textrm{e}^{-4 \mu } + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{-6 \mu } \right) } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tB(ħ)=2μ-2e4ξ+1e-2μ-3e8ξ+2eiħ+4+e-iħe4ξ+3e-4μ+Oe-6μwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = \exp \left( \mu \right) $$\end{document}κ=expμ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ are the complex moduli of the mirror curve (2.1). We will also use3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} t_F\left( \hbar \right) = t_B\left( \hbar \right) - 4 \xi \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tFħ=tBħ-4ξ.If we sent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ħ→0 we recover the classical mirror map relating the Kähler parameters of local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_0$$\end{document}F0, to the complex moduli \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ. For example, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi = 0$$\end{document}ξ=0 the classical mirror map is simply given by3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} t_B \left( 0 \right) = 2 \mu - 4 \, \textrm{e}^{- 2 \mu } \, \, _{4}F_{3}\left[ \left. \begin{array}{c} 1 , \, 1 , \, \frac{3}{2} , \, \frac{3}{2} \\ 2 , \, 2 , \, 2\end{array}\right| \, 16 \, \textrm{e}^{- 2 \mu }\right] \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tB0=2μ-4e-2μ4F31,1,32,322,2,216e-2μ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\, _4F_3$$\end{document}4F3 is the generalized hypergeometric function.

From the perspective of geometric engineering, topological string theory on local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0 engineers a five-dimensional, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}=1$$\end{document}N=1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{SU}\left( 2 \right) $$\end{document}SU2 SYM theory in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω-background [30, 31]. In this gauge theory context, the quantum mirror map corresponds to the inverse of the Wilson loop in the fundamental representation. The latter can be computed via supersymmetric localization [54, 55] and the first few terms read3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W\left( t_F, t_B, \hbar \right)= & \textrm{e}^{t_F/2} + \textrm{e}^{- t_F / 2} + \left[ \frac{\left( \textrm{e}^{t_F/2} + \textrm{e}^{- t_F / 2} \right) }{\left( 1 - It is easily verified that setting3.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} t_B = t_B(\hbar ) \, , \qquad \qquad t_F = t_F(\hbar ) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tB=tB(ħ),tF=tF(ħ),in equation (3.4) gives3.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W\left( t_F(\hbar ), t_B(\hbar ), \hbar \right) = \textrm{e}^{-2 \xi } \kappa \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}WtF(ħ),tB(ħ),ħ=e-2ξκ.One can easily check, at least numerically, that both expansions in (3.1) and in (3.4) are convergent, see e.g. [57, 58].

The closed string sector and the spectral determinant

Let us first review some important elements of the TS/ST correspondence for the closed string sector [3, 4], see [59, 60] for a review. Note that we focus on the specific case where the CY threefold is local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_0$$\end{document}F0.

One feature of the TS/ST correspondence is that, on the topological string side, the relevant quantities involve a special combination of refined topological string partition functions in the GV (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$- \epsilon _1 = \epsilon _2 = g_s$$\end{document}-ϵ1=ϵ2=gs) and NS limit (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _1 \rightarrow 0, \epsilon _2 = \hbar $$\end{document}ϵ1→0,ϵ2=ħ) respectively with the relation3.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g_s = \frac{4 \pi ^2}{\hbar } \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gs=4π2ħ.The arguments of these two sets of special functions are typically rescaled with respect to each other. Hence, it is convenient to define3.8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \alpha ^D=\left( \frac{2\pi }{\hbar } \right) \alpha \,.\end{aligned}$$\end{document}αD=2πħα.The self-dual, or maximally supersymmetric point, is defined at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar =g_s=2\pi $$\end{document}ħ=gs=2π [3, 10].

The main quantity is the closed topological string grand potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(\mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(μ,ξ,ħ). This quantity encapsulates both perturbative and non-perturbative contributions to the closed topological string free energy near the large radius point [3, 57, 61]. F_\textrm{CS}\left( \xi , \hbar \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Aξ,ħ=4ξ33πħ+ħξ12π+Acħπ-FCSξ,ħ,3.11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A_c\left( k \right) = \frac{2 \zeta \left( 3 \right) }{\pi ^2 k} \left( 1 - The specific combination of GV and NS free energies in (3.9) provides a genuinely non-perturbative completion of both quantities. Indeed, the NS and GV functions are not well-defined functions of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s$$\end{document}gs when considered separately. Each of them individually exhibits a dense set of poles along the real \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s$$\end{document}gs line, making them ill-defined. However, these poles cancel in the combinations (3.14) and (3.17), and the resulting function is perfectly well-defined for any value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar $$\end{document}ħ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_s$$\end{document}gs.
