The leading order reads 3.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F^\textrm{NS}_\textrm{inst} \left( t_F , t_B , \hbar \right) = \left[ \frac{\textrm{i}\left( 1 + \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \right) }{\left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{- t_F} \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{- \textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{- t_F} \right) } \right] \textrm{e}^{- t_B} + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{-2 \, t_B} \right) } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FinstNStF,tB,ħ=i1+eiħ1-eiħ1-eiħe-tF1-e-iħe-tFe-tB+Oe-2tB,3.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F^\textrm{GV}_\textrm{inst} \left( t_F , t_B , g_s\right) = \left[ \frac{2 \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}g_s}}{\left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}g_s} \right) ^2 \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{- t_F} \right) ^2} \right] \textrm{e}^{-t_B} + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{- 2 \, t_B} \right) } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FinstGVtF,tB,gs=2eigs1-eigs21-e-tF2e-tB+Oe-2tB, and the all order constructions can be found in appendix B, equations (B.12) and (B.15) respectively.

The instanton part of the grand potential also consists of two parts: one coming from the instanton part of the NS free energy, and one being the instanton part of the GV free energy. Together, they read 3.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{J}_\textrm{inst}(\mu , \xi , \hbar ) = F^\textrm{GV}_\textrm{inst} \left( \left( \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } \right) t_F \left( \hbar \right) , \left( \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } \right) t_B \left( \hbar \right) , \frac{4 \pi ^2}{\hbar } \right) \nonumber \\ + \left( -\frac{1}{2 \pi } + \frac{t_F(\hbar )}{2 \pi }\partial _{t_F} + \frac{t_B(\hbar )}{2 \pi }\partial _{t_B} + \frac{\hbar }{2 \pi }\partial _{\hbar } \right) F^\textrm{NS}_\textrm{inst} \left( t_F(\hbar ), t_B(\hbar ), {\hbar }\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jinst(μ,ξ,ħ)=FinstGV2πħtFħ,2πħtBħ,4π2ħ+-12π+tF(ħ)2π∂tF+tB(ħ)2π∂tB+ħ2π∂ħFinstNStF(ħ),tB(ħ),ħ where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^\textrm{NS}_\textrm{inst}$$\end{document}FinstNS is the instanton part of the 5d Nekrasov free energy in the NS limit, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^\textrm{GV}_\textrm{inst}$$\end{document}FinstGV is similarly the instanton part of the 5d Nekrasov free energy in the GV limit. The leading order reads 3.18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F^\textrm{NS}_\textrm{inst} \left( t_F , t_B , \hbar \right) = \left[ \frac{\textrm{i}\left( 1 + \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \right) }{\left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{- t_F} \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{- \textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{- t_F} \right) } \right] \textrm{e}^{- t_B} + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{-2 \, t_B} \right) } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FinstNStF,tB,ħ=i1+eiħ1-eiħ1-eiħe-tF1-e-iħe-tFe-tB+Oe-2tB,3.19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F^\textrm{GV}_\textrm{inst} \left( t_F , t_B , g_s\right) = \left[ \frac{2 \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}g_s}}{\left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}g_s} \right) ^2 \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{- t_F} \right) ^2} \right] \textrm{e}^{-t_B} + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{- 2 \, t_B} \right) } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FinstGVtF,tB,gs=2eigs1-eigs21-e-tF2e-tB+Oe-2tB, and the all order constructions can be found in appendix B, equations (B.12) and (B.15) respectively.

It is well known that, given an asymptotic series, its non-perturbative completion is not unique; additional conditions are required to fix it. In the context of the TS/ST correspondence, these conditions are provided by the spectral theory of the quantized mirror curve. In particular, the non-perturbative completion defined by the TS/ST correspondence is the unique one that correctly reproduces the spectrum and eigenfunctions of the operator associated with the quantum mirror curve, which in turn is related to an underlying relativistic quantum integrable systems. The same non-perturbative completion was also used in ABJM theory [57, 63], where it successfully captures non-perturbative effects in its corresponding string dual [64]. Nevertheless, other completions are possible in principle; see for instance [65, 66] for an alternative proposal, and [67] for a broader discussion of non-perturbative completions in topological string theory and their interplay with resurgence.

From the perspective of spectral theory, it is natural to consider the grand-canonical ensemble. One of the key statements of the TS/ST correspondence is then that3.20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det \left( 1 + \kappa \rho \right) = \sum _{k \in {\mathbb {Z}}} \textrm{e}^{\textrm{J}\left( \mu + \textrm{i}2 \pi k, \xi , \hbar \right) } \, , \qquad \qquad \kappa = \exp \left( \mu \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det1+κρ=∑k∈ZeJμ+i2πk,ξ,ħ,κ=expμ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ is the operator in (2.12) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}$$\end{document}J is the grand potential of (3.9). An important point is that (3.20) is entire in the full \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ plane, therefore all the singularities in the closed string moduli space are smoothed out, and the full quantity is background independent. To extract the partition function around specific points in the moduli space, we need to expand (3.20) accordingly. For instance, expanding around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = \infty $$\end{document}κ=∞ leads to the large radius expansion. Expanding around the orbifold point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = 0$$\end{document}κ=0 gives [3, 15, 44]3.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \det \left( 1 + \kappa \rho \right) = \sum _{N = 0}^{\infty } \kappa ^N Z\left( N, \hbar \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}det1+κρ=∑N=0∞κNZN,ħ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z(N, \hbar )$$\end{document}Z(N,ħ) is defined in (2.15). It is a distinctive feature of this construction that even though one expands the determinant around the orbifold point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa = 0$$\end{document}κ=0, the coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z(N, \hbar )$$\end{document}Z(N,ħ) actually encode the non-perturbative partition function in the conifold frame.

The open string sector and the eigenfunctions

Let us now turn to the open sector of the topological string. As before, we focus on the example of local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0. The grand potential for the open topological string in the presence of a toric D-brane on an external leg of the toric diagram was introduced in [1, 2]. Here, we follow the formulation in [29, app. A], where the resummation in the open string modulus x is also performed.

The open string grand potential is3.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{J }^\textrm{open}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar ) = \textrm{J }^\textrm{open}_\textrm{p}(x, \xi , \hbar )+\textrm{J }^\textrm{open}_{\text {1-loop}}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar ) + \textrm{J}^\textrm{open}_\textrm{inst}\left( x , \mu , \xi , \hbar \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jopen(x,μ,ξ,ħ)=Jpopen(x,ξ,ħ)+J1-loopopen(x,μ,ξ,ħ)+Jinstopenx,μ,ξ,ħ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu $$\end{document}μ is the closed string modulus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ is the mass parameter, and x is the open string modulus. One can express (3.24) in closed form in terms of elementary functions and the classical dilogarithm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathop \textrm{Li}\nolimits _2$$\end{document}Li2 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in 2 \pi \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈2πQ>0, by using (A.8). This 1-loop part of the grand potential consists again of contributions from both the NS and GV free energies, just as in the closed sector. See [29, app. A] for details.

The instanton part \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}^\textrm{open}_\textrm{inst}$$\end{document}Jinstopen consists also of a part coming from the NS free energy, and a part coming from the GV free energy 3.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \textrm{J}^\textrm{open}_\textrm{inst}\left( x, \mu , \xi , \hbar \right) = F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{NS,inst} \left( x, t_F(\hbar ), t_B(\hbar ), \hbar \right) \nonumber \\ & \quad + F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{GV, inst} \left( \left( \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } \right) x, \left( \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } \right) t_F(\hbar ), \left( \frac{2 \pi }{\hbar } \right) t_B(\hbar ), \frac{4 \pi ^2}{\hbar } \right) \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jinstopenx,μ,ξ,ħ=FNS,instopenx,tF(ħ),tB(ħ),ħ+FGV,openinst2πħx,2πħtF(ħ),2πħtB(ħ),4π2ħ. Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{NS, inst}$$\end{document}FNS,openinst represents the NS limit of the refined open topological string free energy associated with a brane inserted on the outer leg of the toric diagram: 3.26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{NS, inst} \left( x, t_F, t_B, \hbar \right) = \nonumber \\ & \frac{ \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{\frac{t_F}{2} - x} \left( 1 + \textrm{e}^{-t_F} + \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \left( 1 +\textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar }\right) \textrm{e}^{-\frac{t_F}{2}-x} \right) }{\left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{- t_F}\right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{- \textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{-t_F}\right) \left( 1 + \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{\frac{t_F}{2} - x}\right) \left( 1 + \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\hbar } \textrm{e}^{-\frac{t_F}{2} - x}\right) } \textrm{e}^{- t_B} + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{- 2 \, t_B} \right) },\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FNS,openinstx,tF,tB,ħ=eiħetF2-x1+e-tF+eiħ1+eiħe-tF2-x1-eiħ1-eiħe-tF1-e-iħe-tF1+eiħetF2-x1+eiħe-tF2-xe-tB+Oe-2tB, and the all order definition is given in appendix B, equation (B.12). Similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{GV, inst}$$\end{document}FGV,openinst is the open topological string free energy corresponding to a brane inserted in the outer leg of the toric diagram: 3.27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{GV, inst} \left( x, t_F, t_B, g_s \right) = \nonumber \\ & - \frac{\textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{g_s}{2}} \textrm{e}^{\frac{t_F}{2} - x} \left( 1 + \textrm{e}^{-t_F} - 2 \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{g_s}{2}} \textrm{e}^{- \frac{t_F}{2} - x} \right) }{\left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}g_s} \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{-t_F} \right) ^2 \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{g_s}{2}} \textrm{e}^{\frac{t_F}{2} - x} \right) \left( 1 - \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{g_s}{2}} \textrm{e}^{- \frac{t_F}{2} - x} \right) } \textrm{e}^{- t_B} + \mathcal {O}{\left( \textrm{e}^{-2 t_B} \right) },\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}FGV,openinstx,tF,tB,gs=-eigs2etF2-x1+e-tF-2eigs2e-tF2-x1-eigs1-e-tF21-eigs2etF2-x1-eigs2e-tF2-xe-tB+Oe-2tB, and the all order expression for (3.27) can be found in appendix B, equation (B.15).

These poles should be related to the transition from the external to the internal leg of the toric diagram, and they do not disappear in the open string grand potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J }^\textrm{open}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar ) $$\end{document}Jopen(x,μ,ξ,ħ).

Now we want to relate (3.22) to the eigenfunctions of the quantum mirror curve (2.3). It is important to emphasize that there are numerous ways to construct formal solutions to (2.3). For instance, consider3.30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \exp \Bigl [\textrm{J }^\textrm{open}_\textrm{p}(x, \xi , \hbar ) + \textrm{J }^\textrm{open}_{\text {1-loop}}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar ) + F^\textrm{open}_\textrm{NS,inst} \left( x, t_F(\hbar ), t_B(\hbar ), \hbar \right) \Bigr ] \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}exp[Jpopen(x,ξ,ħ)+J1-loopopen(x,μ,ξ,ħ)+FNS,instopenx,tF(ħ),tB(ħ),ħ].While this expression formally satisfies (2.3), it is not a well-defined function for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in \mathbb {R}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈R>0 due to the dense set of poles at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in \pi \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}ħ∈πQ, and it fails to satisfy the analytic properties required by a proper eigenfunction as discussed below (2.3). The exponential of (3.22) on the other hand, as well as each individual term in (3.35), would give a well-defined solution to the difference equation for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in \mathbb {R}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈R>0. However, it does not have the correct analytical properties or asymptotic behaviour to be in the domain of the quantized mirror curve. Hence, it does not qualify as an eigenfunction either.

To construct proper eigenfunctions, it is useful to introduce the full grand potential, which is defined as3.31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} Let us make some comments on this result:The grand potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) is defined in the large-radius frame, corresponding to placing a brane on the outer leg of the toric diagram. It is therefore expected that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) has poles in x: it is associated with a specific patch of the open-string moduli space and is not background independent. Starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) and moving to a different patch in the moduli space requires performing a modular transformation followed by an analytic continuation. In contrast, the non-perturbative completion that we propose — given in equation (3.35) — is an entire function of both the open modulus x and the closed moduli. In this sense, equation (3.35) is background independent: we can simply expand it around any desired point in the moduli space, such as the large-radius or orbifold regions, and directly recover the corresponding open-string partition function valid in that particular frame. At the technical level, such background independence is obtained by the summation over k and the summation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ in (3.33) and (3.35). Indeed, as we reviewed in the introduction, the summation over k in equation (3.20) smooths all the singularities in the closed-string moduli space, parametrized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, yielding an entire function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. The sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ in equation (3.35) plays an analogous role for the open-string modulus x: each of the two terms in (3.33) and (3.35) individually has singularities in x, but these are smoothed out once the two contributions are combined and the sum over k is performed. This property holds even off-shell, i.e. for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \ne -\textrm{e}^{E_n}$$\end{document}κ≠-eEn. Geometrically, it was argued in [1, 2] that such a sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ should correspond to a sum over the two sheets of the mirror curve.As can be seen in Fig. 2 and in appendix D, when evaluated on-shell, the two terms in (3.35) have the same real part along the real line, which is pole-free, while having opposite imaginary parts, with poles that cancel in the sum.An important part of the statement is that the sums in (3.35) are convergent. The instanton expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}_\text {inst}$$\end{document}Jinst and the sum over the shifts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z are both essentially expansions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\exp \left( -t_B\left( \hbar \right) \right) $$\end{document}exp-tBħ, and the mirror map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_B\left( \hbar \right) $$\end{document}tBħ is itself is given as an expansion in large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. Hence, one can think of this as a convergent semi-classical expansion. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in 2 \pi \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈2πQ>0 numerical evidence suggests that the convergence of this series is compact, that is uniform on every compact subset of the complex x-plane, but only pointwise near complex infinity. Similar properties seem to hold for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in 2 \pi \left( \mathbb {R}_{>0} \setminus \mathbb {Q}_{>0} \right) $$\end{document}ħ∈2πR>0\Q>0 as well.Let us end with a few words about the evidence in favour of (3.35). Many tests that do no directly involve the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma = 2$$\end{document}σ=2 term as written in (3.33) were done in [1, 2]. Let us in particular mention the closed form solutions they wrote down for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi = 0$$\end{document}ξ=0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar = 2 \pi $$\end{document}ħ=2π, for which one can explicitly check the pole cancellation. Strong numerical evidence for our proposal (3.35) comes from diagonalizing the quantum mirror curve in the basis of the harmonic oscillator as in [68], and comparing the resulting numerical eigenfunctions with (3.35). See also the explanation at the beginning of appendix D. Another non-trivial test of (3.35) comes from the so-called standard and dual four-dimensional limits. These are interesting stories in their own right, which we will discuss next.

The grand potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) is defined in the large-radius frame, corresponding to placing a brane on the outer leg of the toric diagram. It is therefore expected that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) has poles in x: it is associated with a specific patch of the open-string moduli space and is not background independent. Starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) and moving to a different patch in the moduli space requires performing a modular transformation followed by an analytic continuation. In this sense, equation (3.35) is background independent: we can simply expand it around any desired point in the moduli space, such as the large-radius or orbifold regions, and directly recover the corresponding open-string partition function valid in that particular frame. At the technical level, such background independence is obtained by the summation over k and the summation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ in (3.33) and (3.35). Indeed, as we reviewed in the introduction, the summation over k in equation (3.20) smooths all the singularities in the closed-string moduli space, parametrized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ, yielding an entire function in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. The sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ in equation (3.35) plays an analogous role for the open-string modulus x: each of the two terms in (3.33) and (3.35) individually has singularities in x, but these are smoothed out once the two contributions are combined and the sum over k is performed. This property holds even off-shell, i.e. for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \ne -\textrm{e}^{E_n}$$\end{document}κ≠-eEn. Geometrically, it was argued in [1, 2] that such a sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ should correspond to a sum over the two sheets of the mirror curve.

The grand potential \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) is defined in the large-radius frame, corresponding to placing a brane on the outer leg of the toric diagram. Starting from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}(x, \mu , \xi , \hbar )$$\end{document}J(x,μ,ξ,ħ) and moving to a different patch in the moduli space requires performing a modular transformation followed by an analytic continuation. The sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ in equation (3.35) plays an analogous role for the open-string modulus x: each of the two terms in (3.33) and (3.35) individually has singularities in x, but these are smoothed out once the two contributions are combined and the sum over k is performed. This property holds even off-shell, i.e. for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa \ne -\textrm{e}^{E_n}$$\end{document}κ≠-eEn. Geometrically, it was argued in [1, 2] that such a sum over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ should correspond to a sum over the two sheets of the mirror curve.

As can be seen in Fig. 2 and in appendix D, when evaluated on-shell, the two terms in (3.35) have the same real part along the real line, which is pole-free, while having opposite imaginary parts, with poles that cancel in the sum.

As can be seen in Fig. 2 and in appendix D, when evaluated on-shell, the two terms in (3.35) have the same real part along the real line, which is pole-free, while having opposite imaginary parts, with poles that cancel in the sum.

An important part of the statement is that the sums in (3.35) are convergent. The instanton expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}_\text {inst}$$\end{document}Jinst and the sum over the shifts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z are both essentially expansions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\exp \left( -t_B\left( \hbar \right) \right) $$\end{document}exp-tBħ, and the mirror map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_B\left( \hbar \right) $$\end{document}tBħ is itself is given as an expansion in large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. Hence, one can think of this as a convergent semi-classical expansion. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in 2 \pi \mathbb {Q}_{>0}$$\end{document}ħ∈2πQ>0 numerical evidence suggests that the convergence of this series is compact, that is uniform on every compact subset of the complex x-plane, but only pointwise near complex infinity. Similar properties seem to hold for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \in 2 \pi \left( \mathbb {R}_{>0} \setminus \mathbb {Q}_{>0} \right) $$\end{document}ħ∈2πR>0\Q>0 as well.

An important part of the statement is that the sums in (3.35) are convergent. The instanton expansion of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{J}_\text {inst}$$\end{document}Jinst and the sum over the shifts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z are both essentially expansions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\exp \left( -t_B\left( \hbar \right) \right) $$\end{document}exp-tBħ, and the mirror map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_B\left( \hbar \right) $$\end{document}tBħ is itself is given as an expansion in large \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ. Hence, one can think of this as a convergent semi-classical expansion. Four-Dimensional Limits

It is well-known that topological string theory on local CY manifolds can be used to geometrically engineer four-dimensional \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}=2$$\end{document}N=2 theories [30, 31]. In the case of a local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0 geometry, the corresponding four-dimensional gauge theory is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}=2$$\end{document}N=2, SU(2) SYM. In this section, we study the four-dimensional limit of the open TS/ST correspondence. There are two distinct four-dimensional limits one can implement: the standard limit, discussed in subsect. 4.1, and the dual limit, presented in subsect. 4.2.

The standard four-dimensional limit

Let us consider the mirror curve for local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {F}_0$$\end{document}F0 (2.1). In the standard 4d limit, the parameters of the curve scale as [30]4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} x= R {x}_\textrm{4d}\, , \qquad \textrm{e}^{2 \xi }= \frac{1}{\sqrt{t} R^2} \, , \qquad \kappa = \frac{1}{\sqrt{t}R^2} \left( -2-R^2 E\right) \, , \qquad \hbar = R \epsilon \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x=Rx4d,e2ξ=1tR2,κ=1tR2-2-R2E,ħ=Rϵ,and the limit is taken as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}R→0. In this limit, (2.2) becomes the Fourier transformed modified Mathieu operator4.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{O}_\textrm{FMa}= \sqrt{t} \left( \textrm{e}^{\hat{y}} + \textrm{e}^{-\hat{y} }\right) +\hat{x}^2 \, , \qquad \qquad [\hat{x}, \hat{y}]=\textrm{i}\epsilon \, , \qquad \qquad t, \epsilon > 0 \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}OFMa=tey^+e-y^+x^2,[x^,y^]=iϵ,t,ϵ>0,where we omit the subscripts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm 4d$$\end{document}4d in the variable x for the sake of notation. The energy spectrum is then discrete and the same for both operators.

Result

The standard four-dimensional limit (4.1) was examined in the context of the closed TS/ST correspondence in [69–71]. Let us make some comments on the above result.The finite difference equation (4.3) has extensive families of formal solutions. For instance, both functions (4.14) and (4.13) are solutions to the Fourier transform Mathieu equation (4.3). Each of these functions is meromorphic, with poles located at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ x = \pm \sigma + \textrm{i}\ell $$\end{document}x=±σ+iℓ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}ℓ∈Z. However, what makes the solution (4.16) special is that in the summation, all poles cancel, yielding a final expression that is entire in x, even when evaluated at generic values of the energy. The factor in the square brackets in (4.14) is crucial for this to happen.Although the symmetric structure of the two contributions from (2.48) is lost in the 4d limit, the key feature that remains is that only the sum (4.16) of these two contributions is entire in x.When we evaluate the above eigenfunctions (4.16), (4.14), (4.13) on-shell, i.e. on the locus (4.11), we obtain 4.17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi (x, E_n, t)= & \left( -1 \right) ^n \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} n} t^\frac{\textrm{i}x}{2} \Gamma \left( \textrm{i}\left( - x + \sigma _n \right) \right) \Gamma \left( \textrm{i}\left( - x - \sigma _n \right) \right) Z^{\mathrm {2d/4d}}_\textrm{NS, inst} \left( x, \sigma _n, t \right) \nonumber \\ & + \textrm{e}^{\textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} n} t^{-\frac{\textrm{i}x}{2}} \Gamma \left( \textrm{i}\left( x + \sigma _n \right) \right) \Gamma \left( \textrm{i}\left( x - \sigma _n \right) \right) Z^{\mathrm {2d/4d}}_\textrm{NS, inst} \left( - x, \sigma _n, t \right) \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ(x,En,t)=-1neiπ2ntix2Γi-x+σnΓi-x-σnZNS,2d/4dinstx,σn,t+eiπ2nt-ix2Γix+σnΓix-σnZNS,2d/4dinst-x,σn,t, which is the well-known form of the eigenfunctions obtained in [33, 35, 37, 38, 41]. Notice that the relation between the two terms in (4.17) once again exhibits the same symmetry structure as in (2.48).Let us now consider the asymptotic behaviour of (4.14) and (4.13) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Re}{\left( x \right) } \rightarrow \pm \infty $$\end{document}Rex→±∞ with constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Im}{\left( x \right) }$$\end{document}Imx. The non-trivial asymptotics is determined by the part involving \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma $$\end{document}Γ functions as well as the term inside the square brackets in (4.14). The finite difference equation (4.3) has extensive families of formal solutions. For instance, both functions (4.14) and (4.13) are solutions to the Fourier transform Mathieu equation (4.3). Each of these functions is meromorphic, with poles located at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ x = \pm \sigma + \textrm{i}\ell $$\end{document}x=±σ+iℓ, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}ℓ∈Z. However, what makes the solution (4.16) special is that in the summation, all poles cancel, yielding a final expression that is entire in x, even when evaluated at generic values of the energy. The factor in the square brackets in (4.14) is crucial for this to happen.

Derivation

Here we derive (4.12). We are interested in the standard 4d limit (4.1) of the eigenfunctions as they appear in terms of the gauge theory grand potential in (3.35). The scaling of the complex structure parameters as given in (4.1) corresponds to the following scaling of the Kähler parameters4.21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} t_B\left( \hbar \right) = - {\ln \left( t \hbar ^4 \right) } + 2 \sigma \hbar \, , \qquad \qquad t_F\left( \hbar \right) = 2 \sigma \hbar \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tBħ=-lntħ4+2σħ,tFħ=2σħ,and in addition, the negative sign of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\kappa $$\end{document}κ in (4.1) is reflected in a shift4.22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} t_{B,F}\left( \hbar \right) \rightarrow t_{B,F}\left( \hbar \right) - \textrm{i}2 \pi \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}tB,Fħ→tB,Fħ-i2π.The standard 4d limit is then taking \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ħ→0 from above while keeping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_\textrm{4d}$$\end{document}x4d, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ and t fixed with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Re}{\left( \sigma \right) }, \, t > 0$$\end{document}Reσ,t>0.

We will not keep track of the overall normalization of the 5d eigenfunctions, since we didn’t fix it in the first place. Hence, we can freely choose an overall normalization, and it turns out that normalizing the eigenfunctions (3.35) as4.23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{e}^{-\textrm{J}\left( \mu , \xi , \hbar \right) } \sum _{k \in \mathbb {Z}} \left( \textrm{e}^{{\textrm{J}\left( x, \mu + \textrm{i}\pi \left( 2 k - 1 \right) , \xi , \hbar \right) }} + \textrm{e}^{\frac{\textrm{i}}{\hbar } \frac{\pi ^2}{2} + \frac{\pi x}{\hbar } + {\textrm{J}\left( - x - \textrm{i}\pi , \mu + \textrm{i}\pi \left( 2 k \right) , \xi , \hbar \right) }} \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e-Jμ,ξ,ħ∑k∈ZeJx,μ+iπ2k-1,ξ,ħ+eiħπ22+πxħ+J-x-iπ,μ+iπ2k,ξ,ħ,will be a convenient choice.14

Let us first look at the closed sector, closely following [69, 70]. One gets for the polynomial part of the closed grand potential (3.13)4.24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \textrm{J}_\textrm{p}\left( \mu + \textrm{i}\pi \ell , \xi , \hbar \right) - \textrm{J}_\textrm{p}{\left( \mu , \xi , \hbar \right) } = \nonumber \\ & \frac{\pi \ell ^2 \ln {\left( t \hbar ^4 \right) }}{2 \hbar } - \textrm{i}\frac{\pi ^2 \ell \left( 2 \ell ^2 - 1 \right) }{3 \hbar } - \textrm{i}\ell \sigma \ln {\left( t \hbar ^4 \right) } - 2 \pi \ell ^2 \sigma + \mathcal {O}{\left( \hbar \right) } \,, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jpμ+iπℓ,ξ,ħ-Jpμ,ξ,ħ=πℓ2lntħ42ħ-iπ2ℓ2ℓ2-13ħ-iℓσlntħ4-2πℓ2σ+Oħ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell = 2 k - 1$$\end{document}ℓ=2k-1 for the first term and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell = 2 k$$\end{document}ℓ=2k for the second term. Hence, one can see that the dominating contributions in the sum over the shifts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k \in \mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}k∈Z are given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k=0,1$$\end{document}k=0,1 for the first term and by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k = 0$$\end{document}k=0 term for the second term. One can already note that this gives a trivial closed sector contribution for the second term, due to our normalization. The 4d limit for the 1-loop and instanton part of the closed grand potential can be dealt with as done in [69, 70] and one finds4.25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \textrm{J}{\left( \mu \pm \textrm{i}\pi , \xi , \hbar \right) } - \textrm{J}{\left( \mu , \xi , \hbar \right) } = \nonumber \\ & \frac{\pi }{2} \frac{\ln ( t \hbar ^4 )}{\hbar } \mp \textrm{i}\frac{\pi }{2} \pm \frac{\textrm{i}}{2} \partial _\sigma F_\textrm{NS}^\textrm{4d}{\left( t, \sigma \right) } - \ln {\left( \textrm{e}^{2 \pi \sigma } - \textrm{e}^{- 2 \pi \sigma } \right) } + \mathcal {O}{\left( \hbar \right) } \,. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Jμ±iπ,ξ,ħ-Jμ,ξ,ħ=π2ln(tħ4)ħ∓iπ2±i2∂σFNS4dt,σ-lne2πσ-e-2πσ+Oħ.where the 4d NS free energy is given in (4.9).

Hence, putting all parts of the grand potential for both the closed and open sectors together gives an overall divergent factor4.32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\pi }{2} \frac{\ln \left( t \hbar ^4\right) }{\hbar } - \frac{\textrm{i}\pi ^2}{3 \hbar } - \ln \left( 2 \pi \hbar \right) \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}π2lntħ4ħ-iπ23ħ-ln2πħ,so that, after appropriate normalization, (4.23) reduces to (4.14), (4.13) and (4.16) in the standard 4d limit (4.21).

The dual four-dimensional limit

A few years ago, [17, 32] showed that, starting from the quantum mirror curve (2.7), one can implement another scaling limit to connect with the four-dimensional gauge theory. In this limit, we take4.33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 4\xi = 4 \pi \textrm{i}\sigma - \frac{2\pi }{R} \log \left( R^4 t\right) \, , \qquad \qquad \textrm{e}^{-2 \xi } \kappa = 2 \cos (2\pi \sigma ) \, , \qquad \qquad \hbar = \frac{4\pi ^2}{R} \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}4ξ=4πiσ-2πRlogR4t,e-2ξκ=2cos(2πσ),ħ=4π2R,and send \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}R→0. One of the key differences between the limits (4.1) and (4.33) is that in (4.1), we take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}ħ→0, whereas in (4.33), we take \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hbar \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}ħ→∞. Hence, we refer to (4.33) as the “dual” four-dimensional limit.

Applying the scaling (4.33) and taking the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R\rightarrow 0$$\end{document}R→0 on the operator kernel (2.12) yields the integral operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _\textrm{GV }:~L^2({\mathbb {R}})\rightarrow L^2({\mathbb {R}})$$\end{document}ρGV:L2(R)→L2(R) with kernel [17]4.34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho _\textrm{GV}\left( q, p \right) = \frac{\textrm{e}^{-4 t^{1/4}\cosh q}\textrm{e}^{-4 t^{1/4}\cosh p}}{\cosh \left( \frac{q-p}{2} \right) } \, , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρGVq,p=e-4t1/4coshqe-4t1/4coshpcoshq-p2,and we look for square-integrable eigenfunctions4.35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{{\mathbb {R}}}\textrm{d}p \ \rho _\textrm{GV}(q, p)\widehat{\varphi }_{n}(p, \widehat{E}_n, t)=\widehat{E}_n \widehat{\varphi }_n(q, \widehat{E}_n, t) \, .\end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫RdpρGV(q,p)φ^n(p,E^n,t)=E^nφ^n(q,E^n,t).Interestingly, (4.34) first appeared in the literature in the 1970 s in studies of the 2d Ising model and the theory of Painlevé equations, see [77–80]. We will refer to it as McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator. The connection between the quantum mirror curve (2.7) and the Painlevé kernel (4.34) made it possible to prove the TS/ST correspondence for local \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {F}}_0$$\end{document}F0 in this particular dual 4d limit [17]. See also [20] for the generalization to all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y^{N,0}$$\end{document}YN,0 geometries.

Relating modified Mathieu to McCoy–Tracy–Wu

From the preceding discussion, we see that: The eigenfunctions and spectrum of the (Fourier transformed) modified Mathieu operator (4.4) are determined by gauge theory partition functions in the NS phase of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω background.The eigenfunctions and spectrum of the (Fourier transformed) McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator (4.34) are determined by gauge theory partition functions in the GV phase of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω background.It was first shown in [81], based on [82],16 that these two phases of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega $$\end{document}Ω background can be related by using the Nakajima-Yoshioka blowup equations [84]. This relation was extended to partition functions in the presence of surface defects in [85, 86]. It is therefore natural to ask whether we can relate the eigenfunctions and the spectra of (4.4) and (4.34).

The spectrum

For the modified Mathieu equation the spectrum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_n$$\end{document}En is given in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _n$$\end{document}σn (4.10), which is a solution of (4.11) [74], while for the McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator the spectrum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{E}_n$$\end{document}E^n is given in terms of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{\sigma }_n$$\end{document}σ^n (4.37), which is a solution of (4.40) [17]. The eigenfunctions

One can verify that the modified Mathieu and the McCoy–Tracy–Wu operators commute. Since the modified Mathieu operator possesses a self-adjoint, trace-class inverse, and the McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator is itself self-adjoint and trace-class, it follows that they admit a common \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-orthonormal basis of eigenfunctions. Indeed, we can also check numerically, that4.48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { \frac{\phi (x, E_n, t)}{\phi (x_0, E_n, t)} = \frac{\varphi (x, \widehat{E}_n, t)}{\varphi (x_0, \widehat{E}_n, t)}}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕ(x,En,t)ϕ(x0,En,t)=φ(x,E^n,t)φ(x0,E^n,t)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi ( x, \widehat{E}_n, t )$$\end{document}φ(x,E^n,t) are the Fourier transformed eigenfunctions of the McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator in (4.44), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ( x, E_n, t ) $$\end{document}ϕ(x,En,t) are the Fourier transformed eigenfunctions of the modified Mathieu operator (4.16) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0$$\end{document}x0 is some arbitrary point which is not a zero of the eigenfunctions. The equality (4.48) should follow from taking the NS limit of the blowup equations in the presence of surface defect [85]. A detailed study will appear elsewhere. Note that for (4.48) to hold, we must evaluate both sides on-shell, i.e. at (4.46), (4.11). For generic values of E, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\widehat{E}$$\end{document}E^ (4.48) does not hold.

The operators

Let us now use the relation between the spectrum and the eigenfunctions of the modified Mathieu operator (4.4) and McCoy–Tracy–Wu operator (4.34) to find a relation between the operators themselves. To avoid subtleties related to the domain of definition, it is convenient to work with bounded operators.

We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _\textrm{NS}$$\end{document}ρNS the trace class inverse of the modified Mathieu operator184.49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} - \frac{1}{4}\partial _q^2+\sqrt{t}(\textrm{e}^{2q}+\textrm{e}^{-2q}) \, . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}-14∂q2+t(e2q+e-2q).As discussed previously, the modified Mathieu operator and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{\textrm{GV}}$$\end{document}ρGV admit a common basis of eigenfunctions. Consequently, the same holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{\textrm{NS}}$$\end{document}ρNS and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _{\textrm{GV}}$$\end{document}ρGV. Hence, by employing their spectral decompositions together with the relation between their spectra given in (4.47), it follows immediately that4.50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { \rho _\textrm{NS} = {\mathfrak {F}}\left( \rho _\textrm{GV} \right) \, , } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρNS=FρGV,4.51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \boxed { \mathfrak {F} \ : \ \Sigma \left( \rho _\textrm{GV} \right) \rightarrow \Sigma \left( \rho _\textrm{NS} \right) \ : \ E \mapsto \left( -
